### 4. Decisions and questions asked of Taylor

#### 4.1 Start checks
- `lease-clauses.csv` decompressed to 73,353,243 bytes, SHA-256 `903498ba…acf98`, matching the prompt. 18,439 rows (18,327 active); 181 active rows carry NY. `missing-topics-NY.md` lists 199 topics. All match.
- Old output copies were deleted at the start. The workspace survived a container restart, so no re-attachment was needed.

#### 4.2 Class decisions
Each canvass helper worked from the same 18 numbered class decisions: scope, article 6-A, Multiple Dwelling Law and Multiple Residence Law scope, local law, case law, NEEDS_REVIEW, fees, deposits, waivers, void-term penalties, exculpation, tag-first, answered-elsewhere, absences, not located, options, federal and court rules. Rows cite them as "NY gaps log §4 class decision N". They restate Taylor's standing decisions and the SOP; none is new policy. The text is reproduced at the end of this section.

#### 4.3 Named items
- **Item 9, three central shared edits** (confirmed; details in §6 "From MAIN"):
  - `smoking-policy`: confirmed; New York uses `smoking-policy-ny`. Finding: `smoking-policy-ny`'s cost sentence should name Tenant, an occupant, or a guest or invitee of Tenant (N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 235-b(1); N.Y. Mult. Dwell. Law § 78(1); N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 174).
  - `no-alterations`: confirmed as tagged.
  - `addendum-precedence`: confirmed. Its required-by-law exception covers every New York required notice; a note-only addition is proposed.
- **Item 9, `hoa-compliance`:** fits as tagged; no `-ny` override. A note-only addition is proposed (N.Y. Real Prop. Acts. Law § 702(1)).
- **Item 10(a):** `early-termination-ny` is proposed in §6 with every field, superseding `early-termination-ks` for New York: a flat one-month fee, RECOMMENDED. The "vacates or abandons the property without notifying Landlord" limb is dropped (N.Y. Real Prop. Acts. Law §§ 711, 768; N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 216(1)). Claude Code applies it at sync.
- **Item 10(b), casualty fault persons:** confirmed. `casualty-termination-ny` and `tenant-caused-damage-ny` name the same persons for each purpose:
  - casualty exit: Tenant, household member or guest, a written variation N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 227 allows;
  - abatement: "persons under Tenant's direction or control", as N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 235-b(1) puts it;
  - repair cost: household member or guest, as N.Y. Mult. Dwell. Law § 78(1) and N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 174 put it.

  No wording change (§6 "From B_waivers_habitability_repairs").
- **Item 10(c), CARES:** confirmed. No NY eviction row says New York's notices are the only notices that can apply.
- **Item 11, arbitration:** not offered. `edu-arbitration-ny` records why.
  - The law allows arbitration generally (N.Y. Civ. Prac. L. & R. 7501).
  - Its reach into a residential lease turns on case law: N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 259-c (jury waivers in property-damage claims), N.Y. Civ. Prac. L. & R. 7515 (discrimination claims, subject to federal law) and N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 399-c (consumer contracts).
  - So does the penalty for including the clause: N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 349(h) gives damages and attorney's fees for a deceptive practice.
  - Per Taylor's rule, `arbitration-agreement` is not tagged NY. Battery NYG-1 found no outright statutory bar.
- **Item 4, CPLR 3218 (`edu-confession-of-judgment-ny`):** read whole. A confession of judgment taken before a claim arises is not barred for a residential lease, but it is limited:
  - it must be a separate sworn affidavit;
  - it must be filed within three years in the county where the tenant lives;
  - it can't be entered after the tenant's death;
  - for sums not yet due, execution issues only for what has become due;
  - it gives a money judgment, never possession.

  The library doesn't offer it (NY log §6.1). The FTC Credit Practices Rule (FEDERAL: 16 C.F.R. § 444.2) binds lenders and retail installment sellers extending credit, which a landlord renting a home is not (Claude's reading). It was saved, and no row relies on it.

#### 4.4 Decisions made in this pass
- **Two new optional clauses** (rule 54): `promised-repairs-ny` (CONDITIONAL) and `designated-repairer-ny` (RECOMMENDED). Neither is in NY log §6.1. Both are offered, following the MO, UT, IL, MI, NM, MN and OH passes, each with a New York savings sentence. NY log §6.1 names neither option as declined, and recent passes settle the question, so Taylor was not asked (rule 76).
- **Mid-lease rent escalation (`rent-escalation`):** no escalation clause is offered. The 2026-10-03 log found that no statute gives a right to an escalation term, so it is not a rule 54 option. Another consideration: N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 223-b raises a presumption of retaliation when a landlord changes the terms of a tenancy within a year of a good-faith complaint. `edu-rent-escalation-ny` says a fixed schedule can be written into the rent term by hand, and that where article 6-A applies, increases above the local rent standard are presumed unreasonable. Taylor wasn't asked (rule 76): the log's finding settles it.
- **Pass-through charges under article 6-A:** `government-fee-reimbursement-ny` and `tax-escalation-ny` are offered with a bracketed warning that where the Good Cause Eviction Law applies the charge may count as 'rent' (N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 211(5)), so the yearly increase test applies. `unbundled-parking-ny` relies on the statute's own exclusion of separate parking charges. The warning is kept rather than the clauses dropped.
- **Shared rows declined in favour of `-ny` versions** (class decision 12):
  - `tenant-caused-fines-as`: its carve-out would swallow tenant-caused multiple-dwelling violations. `governmental-fines-ny` is used instead.
  - `fees-and-charges-vi`: replaced by `fees-and-charges-ny`, whose prompt lists the barred fees.
  - `tax-insurance-escalation-la`: replaced by `tax-escalation-ny`.
  - `unbundled-parking-ut` and `unbundled-parking-mi`: the charge would start with the first payment, which N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 238-a(1)(a) bars. `unbundled-parking-ny` is used instead.
- **Owner move-in termination (`owner-move-in-reservation`):** declined (`edu-owner-move-in-ny`). Where article 6-A applies, owner occupancy is good cause only within the limits of N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 216(1)(g). Elsewhere, whether a lease reservation is enforceable is case law. The shared `owner-occupancy-termination-ar` variants are not tagged.
- **`no-liens-ny`** is offered as a new optional clause instead of a tag. Every shared version carried either a cost-recovery sentence that could reach legal fees (N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 234-a) or another state's statutory concept.
- **Veterans-only concession:** not offered (`edu-no-veterans-incentive-ny`). New York makes military status a protected class in housing and has no safe harbour for veteran preferences.
- **`employee-housing-ny`:** offered as written. It records the agreed time for possession under N.Y. Real Prop. Acts. Law § 713(11) and promises court process either way, because whether a rent-paying lessee is an employee-occupant is for case law.
- **New optional clauses from batch D:** `family-child-care-consent-ny`, `employee-housing-ny` and `extended-absence-notice-ny`, each with New York conditions in the text. `extended-absence-notice-ks` stays untagged, as NY log §2.2 decided.
- **Disability accommodation notice (N.Y. Exec. Law § 170-d):** the statute tells the Division of Human Rights to require, by regulation, that housing providers give tenants written notice of their rights to request reasonable modifications and accommodations within 30 days of the tenancy's start. The regulation (9 NYCRR Part 466) is on govt.westlaw.com and wasn't read. `edu-accommodation-notice-ny` is proposed in §6 with every field, NEEDS_REVIEW until the regulation is read (class decision 6), rather than added as a delta row now: it is a missing landlord requirement on a topic New York already answers.
- **`condo-unit-particulars-ny`:** offered as CONDITIONAL with basis REQUIRED_DISCLOSURE: N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 339-o ('Deeds and leases of units shall include the following particulars'), for leases of condominium units. The section names no lease length and no case law was searched; including the particulars is the cautious course.
- **`communal-land-lease-approval`:** Present, not the one-line absence the prompt expected. The Indian Law has approval and void-lease rules for leases of nation lands (`edu-tribal-land-lease-approval-ny`). The Indian Law was added to the citation tool's law names so those cites validate.
- **Required-disclosures catalog:** `edu-required-disclosures-ny` also lists `smoke-detector-duties-ny`, `condo-unit-particulars-ny` and N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 396-d, which the prompt's list did not name.
- **`shared-meter-agreement-ny`:** offered as CONDITIONAL with basis CONSTRAINED_TERM. Its bracket states the three conditions of N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 52(2)(b)(i) and (1)(f)-(g). The commission's further minimal-service and extraordinary-cost rules are in 16 NYCRR, which wasn't read; the bracket names them without stating them, and the row stays VERIFIED because the statute states the controlling rule (class decision 6).
- **`utility-shutoff-notice-authorization-co` tagged:** it is an optional authorization the tenant gives. N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 40(1) requires utilities to 'permit a residential customer to designate a third party', and the section is headed "Voluntary third-party notification". The clause doesn't stop the tenant from receiving notices, and it says nothing requires the provider to notify Landlord. Whether a designation made as a lease term is "voluntary" is for case law (not searched), and the optional bracket leaves the choice to the parties.
- **Required lease text found in batch F:** `indoor-air-test-notice-ny` (N.Y. Envtl. Conserv. Law § 27-2405(3): prescribed bold notice in at least 12-point type on the lease's first page, where indoor-air testing applies) and `environmental-easement-ny` (N.Y. Envtl. Conserv. Law § 71-3605(4)). Both are CONDITIONAL. `edu-required-disclosures-ny` (batch E) does not yet list them: added under §5, rows edited after the last check round.
- **EV charger clause:** not offered. Billing a tenant for charger electricity turns on the submetering rules (16 NYCRR Part 96, not read), and no statute gives a market-rate tenant a right to install one. If Taylor wants a clause before Part 96 is read, it should wire the charger to the tenant's own meter and drop any reimbursement the landlord calculates.
- **Towing notice (N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 399-v):** the statute doesn't exclude a lot used only by a building's tenants, and no case law was searched. The rows advise posting the notice before any tow or boot, which is the cautious course. A rewrite of `edu-towing-ny` is proposed in §6.
- **Rooftop solar (N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 342):** the section bars homeowners' associations from rules that 'effectively prohibit' rooftop systems. It doesn't say whether a lease is a 'restriction on use', so the tagged `portable-solar-mo` (written consent, with conditions) is kept rather than a flat ban.

#### 4.5 Questions asked of Taylor
- **2026-11-15, Westlaw for NYCRR.** Asked to approve govt.westlaw.com for 16 NYCRR Part 96, 19 NYCRR and later 9 NYCRR Part 466. Taylor approved ("Go for it on westlaw").
  - Westlaw needed an approval for every load and action, and its browser check blocked script reads. Claude stopped after about ten approvals with no text read, and laid out the choices.
  - Taylor said continue. Justia then showed a Cloudflare check Taylor couldn't clear, so the regulations were read from Cornell LII instead (§1.1, §1.5).
- **Proposed lease clause `accommodation-notice-ny`** (§6, from E): REQUIRED_DISCLOSURE under 9 NYCRR 466.15 and N.Y. Exec. Law § 170-d, reproducing the Division's model notice.
  - The rule requires a written notice within 30 days of the tenancy's start. The lease is one vehicle for it, not the only one.
  - Proposed as REQUIRED, matching `good-cause-notice-ny`. Taylor may prefer it optional with the education row carrying the duty; his call at sync.

#### 4.6 Class decisions (text as given to the canvass)

1. **Scope.** The NY rows cover market-rate residential tenancies under New York State law. Rent-stabilized and rent-controlled units are out of scope; `edu-scope-ny` says so. Where a topic's answer differs for regulated units, say in one sentence that regulated units follow their own rules (`edu-scope-ny`) and don't research them.
2. **Good Cause Eviction Law.** N.Y. Real Prop. Law art. 6-A (§§ 210-218) applies in New York City and in localities that opt in by local law, with the exemptions in `edu-for-cause-eviction-ny`. Where an answer depends on whether article 6-A applies, give both answers and say which applies where. Don't research the opt-in list or local thresholds. Article 6-A and N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 231-c are repealed June 15, 2034 (the sections print "*NB Repealed June 15, 2034"). Every New York lease carries the § 231-c notice (`good-cause-notice-ny`).
3. **Place-limited state statutes are in scope, with the place stated.** The Multiple Dwelling Law applies to cities of 325,000 or more and to localities that adopt it (N.Y. Mult. Dwell. Law § 3(1)-(2)). The Multiple Residence Law applies to 'all cities of less than three hundred twenty-five thousand population and to all towns and villages' (N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 3(1)). Quote each section's own scope words; never merge two sections' wording into one paraphrase.
4. **Local law is named, not researched (rule 3).** That covers the New York City Administrative Code, Housing Maintenance Code and Health Code (window guards are N.Y.C. Health Code § 131.15), Local Law 1 of 2004 (lead), the FARE Act, Local Law 18 of 2022 (short-term rentals), Fair Chance for Housing, and every other city, town or village code. Name it in the row as local law the library does not research.
5. **Case law was not searched** (NY log §1.4). Where only a case could settle a point, the row states the doubt and the cautious course and stays VERIFIED (SOP Part 5 NEEDS_REVIEW policy). Write "no New York case law was searched (NY gaps log §1)".
6. **NEEDS_REVIEW only when a primary source can't be reached.** Examples: an NYCRR part or a federal section the main pass couldn't fetch. Put the fetch request in your fetch file first. The main pass fetches it and resumes you before the row ships.
7. **Fees.** N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 238-a(1)(a): 'Except in instances where statutes or regulations provide for a payment, fee or charge', no landlord may 'demand any payment, fee, or charge for the processing, review or acceptance of an application, or demand any other payment, fee or charge before or at the beginning of the tenancy, except background checks and credit checks as provided by paragraph (b)'. Late fees: N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 238-a(2). Returned-check fees: N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 238-a(2-a). Waivers are void: N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 238-a(3). Legal services, including 'administrative fees', may be assessed only 'pursuant to a court order', and contrary agreements are void (N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 234-a(a)-(c)). 'No fees, charges or penalties other than rent may be sought in a summary proceeding' (N.Y. Real Prop. Acts. Law § 702(1)). Any fee a row describes must be one New York allows. Open each section and quote its own words; these quotes are a guide, not a substitute.
8. **Deposits.** N.Y. Gen. Oblig. Law art. 7 (§§ 7-101 to 7-109). Subdivision 1-a of N.Y. Gen. Oblig. Law § 7-108 sets the rules: (a) one-month cap on deposits and advances; (b) a closed list of retentions; (c) the move-in inspection; (d) the pre-move-out inspection; (e) the itemized statement within 14 days; (g) liability for actual damages, and punitive damages of up to twice the deposit for a willful violation. Waivers are void (N.Y. Gen. Oblig. Law §§ 7-103(3), 7-108(3)). Subdivision 1-a does not apply in rent-controlled units, continuing care retirement communities and the other listed facilities.
9. **Waiver and "cannot be waived" statements.** Before you write that something cannot be waived, find that section's own non-waiver sentence and quote it, not a neighbouring section's. Record what is voided, in the statute's words. A waiver of a rule New York does not have is empty, not lawful: state the default rule first.
10. **Using a void term.** New York has no single penalty for including a void term (`edu-knowing-use-ny`). These make using one costly: N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 223-b(5-a), N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 227-c(6)(a), N.Y. Gen. Oblig. Law § 7-108(1-a)(g), N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 349 (as amended by the FAIR Business Practices Act, L. 2025, ch. 708, effective February 17, 2026), and N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 235-c (unconscionability). Point to `edu-prohibited-terms-ny`, `edu-knowing-use-ny` or `edu-unconscionability-ny` rather than restating them.
11. **Exculpation.** N.Y. Gen. Oblig. Law § 5-321 voids a lease term exempting the lessor from liability for its own negligence. A shared clause with a "Landlord is not liable" sentence that reaches the landlord's own negligence is not tagged NY (use the variants without the disclaimer, rule 52).
12. **Tag first (rules 26-28).** Before writing a row, list every active shared row on the topic (`lib.py topic <key>`, `lib.py shared <key>`). Tag one only if all of these hold:
    - it is lawful in New York as written;
    - its basis does not rest on another state's statute or statutory absence;
    - it neither imposes a limit New York lacks nor omits or gives away a right New York gives the landlord;
    - every fee or unilateral change it allows fits N.Y. Real Prop. Law §§ 238-a, 234-a and 235-g.

    A tag changes only `states` and adds an `NY:` segment. If a shared clause is lawful only on a condition, write an `-ny` row with the condition in its text, or decline the tag; never carry the condition only in the note. Record declined tags in your answers file with the reason.
13. **"Answered elsewhere" still gets its own row.** A topic whose substance sits in an existing NY row still gets a short education row keyed to its own `topic_key` (rule 27 [LA 5]). The row states New York's answer in a sentence or two and points to the other row by id in backticks.
14. **Absences.** A Confirmed-absent row needs a battery whose positives all passed (`{B:NYG-n}` in the notes). The body names the corpus searched ("the Consolidated Laws of New York and the New York Constitution"). Never write "New York has no law on X" when only some laws were searched.
15. **Not located.** Where the subject would live in a source the pass does not hold (an NYCRR part, local codes, case law), the verdict is Not located. The row's body and notes name the source and the section or part that would answer it, say what was proved inside the corpus, and say what applies in its place. Put the source in your fetch file in case the main pass can reach it.
16. **Option offered or declined (rule 54).** A lawful option the library offers gets a LEASE_CLAUSE row. A lawful option the library declines gets an education row: it says the option exists, why the library doesn't offer it, and that the landlord may add their own clause. The options in NY log §6.1 stand; don't reopen them.
17. **Federal law** is cited `FEDERAL: 42 U.S.C. § 3603(b)` in bodies and notes alike. You can't fetch federal text yourself. Put the section in your fetch file, and the main pass saves it to `/home/claude/nyg/sources/federal/`. Only quote federal text that is saved there.

#### From A_deposits_fees_rent

#### Findings on existing rows: batch A_deposits_fees_rent (2026-11-15)

##### 1. `edu-certificate-of-occupancy-ny`: Multiple Residence Law counterpart left out (an addition, not an error)

The row's body says: "In a Multiple Dwelling Law building, you cannot recover rent, or evict for nonpayment, for any period the building is occupied without a required certificate of occupancy." Its notes say: "The Multiple Residence Law's counterpart was not relied on".

The Multiple Residence Law has the same bar for buildings it covers (cities under 325,000, towns and villages):
- N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 302-a(1): 'If any dwelling or structure be occupied in whole or in part for human habitation in violation of section three hundred two of this article, no rent shall be recovered by the owner of such premises for said period, and no action for possession of said premises for nonpayment of such rent shall be maintained therefor.'
- N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 302(1): 'No multiple dwelling shall be occupied in whole or in part until the issuance of a certificate by the department that said dwelling conforms in all respects to the requirements of this chapter', with the pre-1952 exceptions in the same subdivision.

Proposed body sentence (replacing the last sentence): "In a multiple dwelling covered by the Multiple Dwelling Law or the Multiple Residence Law, you cannot recover rent, or evict for nonpayment, for any period the building is occupied without a certificate of occupancy that law requires."
Proposed notes addition: "N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 302-a(1) ('no rent shall be recovered by the owner of such premises for said period'), for occupation in violation of N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 302(1)." The new `edu-rent-demand-bar-ny` (this batch) already states both bars and the rent-impairing-violation bars (N.Y. Mult. Dwell. Law § 302-a; N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 305-a), so this edit is optional.

#### From B_waivers_habitability_repairs

#### Findings on existing rows: B_waivers_habitability_repairs

##### Item 10(b) casualty fault persons

Conclusion: `casualty-termination-ny` and `tenant-caused-damage-ny` name the same persons for the same purpose. No wording change proposed.

Statutes (read whole 2026-11-15 from the saved corpus):
- N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 227: the tenant may surrender 'if the destruction or injury occurred without his or her fault or neglect', and only where 'no express agreement to the contrary has been made in writing'.
- N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 235-b(1): 'When any such condition has been caused by the misconduct of the tenant or lessee or persons under his direction or control, it shall not constitute a breach of such covenants and warranties.' N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 235-b(2) voids any agreement 'waiving or modifying' the tenant's rights.
- N.Y. Mult. Dwell. Law § 78(1): 'the tenant also shall be liable if a violation is caused by his own wilful act, assistance or negligence or that of any member of his family or household or his guest'.
- N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 174: 'the tenant shall also be liable if a violation is caused by his own wilful act, assistance or negligence or that of any member of his family or household or his guests'.

How the two clauses line up, purpose by purpose:
1. Casualty exit (N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 227). `casualty-termination-ny`: Tenant may end the Lease "if the damage occurred through no fault or neglect of Tenant, a member of Tenant's household or Tenant's guest". `tenant-caused-damage-ny`: the exit "does not apply where the damage occurred through the fault or neglect of Tenant, a household member or a guest". Same three persons, same purpose. The statute names only the tenant; the extension to household members and guests is an 'express agreement to the contrary ... in writing' that N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 227 allows. Consistent.
2. Rent abatement (N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 235-b(1)). `tenant-caused-damage-ny`: Rent "will not abate for a condition caused by the misconduct of Tenant or of persons under Tenant's direction or control". `casualty-termination-ny` reduces Rent for unusable parts "except as the Damage Caused by Tenant Section provides", so it borrows the same persons by cross-reference. Both track the statute's own exception and go no further, which N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 235-b(2) requires. Consistent. (Whether a guest is a person "under Tenant's direction or control" is case law, not searched; the clauses rightly do not decide it.)
3. Repair cost (N.Y. Mult. Dwell. Law § 78(1); N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 174). `tenant-caused-damage-ny`: "Tenant, a member of Tenant's household or Tenant's guest" pays the reasonable repair cost; `landlord-maintenance-ny` uses the same three persons ("the misconduct, wilful act or negligence of Tenant, a member of Tenant's household or Tenant's guest"). The statutes say "family or household"; a family member who is not in the household and visits is a guest, so nothing is lost (Claude's reading). `casualty-termination-ny` does not deal with repair cost. Consistent.

The casualty exit and the abatement intentionally use different person sets (household and guest for the exit, direction or control for abatement), because N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 227 may be varied in writing and N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 235-b may not. That difference is correct and should stay.

##### `edu-tenant-repair-remedies-ny`: rent bars not mentioned (optional addition)

The row lists the warranty, the N.Y. Real Prop. Acts. Law § 755 stay, article 7-D, the rent deposit proceedings and the utility offset, but not three statutes that stop rent itself. They are now stated in the new `edu-landlord-breach-remedy-ny`, so nothing is lost; at sync Claude Code may add one pointer sentence to the existing row.
- N.Y. Mult. Dwell. Law § 302-a(1): 'The provisions of this section shall apply to all cities with a population of four hundred thousand or more.' N.Y. Mult. Dwell. Law § 302-a(3)(a): after a rent-impairing violation is not cancelled 'within six months after the date of such notice of such violation, then for the period that such violation remains uncorrected after the expiration of said six months, no rent shall be recovered by any owner'.
- N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 305-a(1): the same rule 'shall apply to all cities of less than five hundred thousand population and to all towns and villages'.
- N.Y. Soc. Serv. Law § 143-b(5)(b): against a welfare recipient, the landlord 'shall not be entitled to an order or judgment awarding him possession of the premises or providing for removal of the tenant, or to a money judgment against the tenant, on the basis of non-payment of rent for any period during which there was outstanding any violation of law relating to dangerous or hazardous conditions or conditions detrimental to life or health'.

Proposed sentence to append to the bodyText of `edu-tenant-repair-remedies-ny`: "Some statutes also bar rent itself, for long-uncorrected rent-impairing violations in multiple dwellings and for welfare recipients while a reported dangerous violation is outstanding (see the row on tenant remedies for landlord breach)."
Proposed notes addition: "Rent bars: N.Y. Mult. Dwell. Law § 302-a; N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 305-a; N.Y. Soc. Serv. Law § 143-b (`edu-landlord-breach-remedy-ny`)."

##### `edu-certificate-of-occupancy-ny`: Multiple Residence Law counterpart confirmed

The row's notes say "The Multiple Residence Law's counterpart was not relied on". It exists and was read whole on 2026-11-15: N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 302-a(1): 'If any dwelling or structure be occupied in whole or in part for human habitation in violation of section three hundred two of this article, no rent shall be recovered by the owner of such premises for said period, and no action for possession of said premises for nonpayment of such rent shall be maintained therefor.' (revision 2019-11-08). The new `edu-condemned-premises-rent-bar-ny` relies on it. Proposed bodyText change for `edu-certificate-of-occupancy-ny`, last sentence: "In a building covered by the Multiple Dwelling Law, or a multiple dwelling covered by the Multiple Residence Law, you cannot recover rent, or evict for nonpayment, for any period the building is occupied without a required certificate of occupancy." Proposed notes change: replace "The Multiple Residence Law's counterpart was not relied on" with "Multiple Residence Law: N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 302-a(1) (quoted in `edu-condemned-premises-rent-bar-ny`)."

#### From D_dv_fairhousing_occupancy

#### Findings on existing NY rows (batch D_dv_fairhousing_occupancy)

##### `edu-no-dv-lockchange-ny` (N.Y. Fam. Ct. Act § 842 now read; topic `dv-protection-order-chapter-moved`)
- Row's notes: "Orders of protection: N.Y. Fam. Ct. Act § 842 (not read)." The section has now been read whole (revisions listed through 2025-12-26).
- Row's body: "changing locks to keep out a co-tenant without a court order (such as an order of protection that excludes them) risks unlawful eviction."
- Statute: an order of protection 'may require the petitioner or the respondent: (a) to stay away from the home, school, business or place of employment of any other party' (N.Y. Fam. Ct. Act § 842(a)); '(d) to permit a designated party to enter the residence during a specified period of time in order to remove personal belongings' (N.Y. Fam. Ct. Act § 842(d)); '(k) 1. to refrain from remotely controlling any connected devices affecting the home, vehicle or property of the person protected by the order' (N.Y. Fam. Ct. Act § 842(k)). Unlawful eviction bars changing the lock 'without supplying the occupant with a key' 'except to the extent permitted by law pursuant to a warrant of eviction or other order of a court of competent jurisdiction or a governmental vacate order' (N.Y. Real Prop. Acts. Law § 768(1)(a), (1)(a)(iii)).
- Problem: the parenthetical implies that an order of protection excluding the co-tenant makes a landlord's lock change lawful. A § 842 order is directed at the petitioner or respondent, not the landlord; whether a stay-away order is an 'other order of a court of competent jurisdiction' under N.Y. Real Prop. Acts. Law § 768(1)(a) that lets the landlord change the lock without giving the excluded co-tenant a key is not settled by statute (case law not searched). The row's conclusion (no lock-change statute) is unaffected.
- Proposed body sentence: "Changing locks to keep out a co-tenant risks unlawful eviction unless a court order allows it. An order of protection tells the abuser to stay away from the home (and can bar them from remotely controlling smart locks or other connected devices), but it does not itself tell you to change the locks or end the abuser's tenancy, so read its terms and get legal advice before changing a lock."
- Proposed notes addition: "N.Y. Fam. Ct. Act § 842 read whole 2026-11-15: an order 'may require the petitioner or the respondent' (N.Y. Fam. Ct. Act § 842) to 'stay away from the home' (N.Y. Fam. Ct. Act § 842(a)) and 'to refrain from remotely controlling any connected devices affecting the home' (N.Y. Fam. Ct. Act § 842(k)); it says nothing of landlords or locks. Whether a stay-away order is an 'other order of a court of competent jurisdiction' (N.Y. Real Prop. Acts. Law § 768(1)(a)) is for case law, not searched (NY gaps log §1). See `edu-no-dv-protection-order-move-ny`."
- Also noted (not a defect): N.Y. Crim. Proc. Law § 530.12, N.Y. Crim. Proc. Law § 530.13, N.Y. Dom. Rel. Law § 240, N.Y. Fam. Ct. Act § 446, N.Y. Fam. Ct. Act § 656 and N.Y. Fam. Ct. Act § 1056 let the court authorize the protected party 'to terminate a lease or rental agreement pursuant to section two hundred twenty-seven-c of the real property law' (N.Y. Crim. Proc. Law § 530.12); N.Y. Fam. Ct. Act § 842 has no such sentence. No existing NY row mentions these court authorizations; `edu-no-dv-protection-order-move-ny` now does.

##### 1. Missing landlord requirement: the reasonable-accommodation notice (N.Y. Exec. Law § 170-d), topic `disability-accommodation`

No NY row states this duty (`lib.py grep '170-d'` returns nothing). The statute, read whole (revisions listed 2020-12-18, 2021-04-02):

'The division of human rights shall promulgate regulations requiring every housing provider under subdivisions two-a and five of section two hundred ninety-six of this chapter, to provide notice to all tenants and prospective tenants, in writing, within thirty days of the effective date of their tenancy, or thirty days from the effective date of this section for current tenants, of their rights to request reasonable modifications and accommodations pursuant to subparagraphs one, two and three of paragraph d of subdivision two-a of section two hundred ninety-six of this chapter and subdivision eighteen of section two hundred ninety-six of this chapter' (N.Y. Exec. Law § 170-d).

`edu-disability-accommodation-ny` states the accommodation and modification rights (N.Y. Exec. Law § 296(18)) but not the notice. Rewritten 2026-11-15 after 9 NYCRR Part 466 was read (Cornell LII, saved in `sources/nycrr/`): the regulation is 9 NYCRR 466.15, with posting by reference to 9 NYCRR 466.3. The proposal is now VERIFIED (it was NEEDS_REVIEW under NY gaps log §4 class decision 6 only because the regulation was unread). ###### 1a. Education row

- title: Notice of the Right to Request Accommodations
- content_type: LANDLORD_EDUCATION
- rule_type: RECOMMENDED
- lease_clause_basis: (blank)
- topic_key: disability-accommodation
- bodyText:

```text
Every owner, managing agent, sublandlord or other housing provider in New York must give each tenant a written notice of the right to request reasonable modifications and reasonable accommodations for a disability, within 30 days after the tenancy begins. Tenants already in place when the law took effect on March 2, 2021 were due it within 30 days after that date; if you have tenants who never got it, for example in a building you bought, the cautious course is to give it to them now. The notice must be in writing, in 12-point type or larger (or another easily legible type), and must give the telephone number and e-mail address of the property manager or other person who accepts accommodation requests. You may deliver it by e-mail, text, electronic messaging, fax or on paper, and you may include it in or with the lease or other papers you routinely give tenants; on paper it must be in the document itself or in an accompanying one. A message that only links to the notice is enough if it says the link is about tenants' rights to reasonable accommodations for persons with disabilities, and the notice must be printable and downloadable. Telling the tenant orally does not count, and neither does posting the notice. Posting is a separate duty: you must also post the notice conspicuously at the property, in an easily accessible, well-lighted place where people seeking housing can readily see it, and any website you create and maintain as a housing provider must prominently display on its homepage a link to the Division of Human Rights' notice. The model notice printed in the Division's regulation is deemed sufficient; `accommodation-notice-ny` puts it in the lease. If a licensed real estate broker or salesperson handles your listing, the broker must see that the notice goes to prospective tenants at the first substantive contact. The regulation states no exemption; whether the Human Rights Law's exemptions for an owner-occupied two-family house and for rooms in an owner's home carry over is unsettled, so the cautious course is to give the notice anyway. The rights themselves are in `edu-disability-accommodation-ny`. Local laws may add notices that these rows do not research.
```

- notes:

```text
NY: Present: 9 NYCRR 466.15 (Division of Human Rights) read whole (formerly NEEDS_REVIEW with the regulation unread). Statute: 'The division of human rights shall promulgate regulations requiring every housing provider under subdivisions two-a and five of section two hundred ninety-six of this chapter, to provide notice to all tenants and prospective tenants, in writing, within thirty days of the effective date of their tenancy, or thirty days from the effective date of this section for current tenants, of their rights to request reasonable modifications and accommodations pursuant to subparagraphs one, two and three of paragraph d of subdivision two-a of section two hundred ninety-six of this chapter and subdivision eighteen of section two hundred ninety-six of this chapter' (N.Y. Exec. Law § 170-d); 'Executive Law section 170-d was effective March 2, 2021' (9 NYCRR 466.15(b)). Who: 'Housing providers that are the owner, lessee, sub-lessee, assignee, or managing agent of a housing accommodation or publicly-assisted housing accommodation, must provide notice, as provided for in this regulation, to all new and current tenants in the following manner' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)). When: 'Within 30 days of the effective date of their tenancy' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)(i)); 'for current tenants, within thirty days after the effective date of Executive Law section 170-d' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)(ii)); giving it now to tenants who never received it is a cautious practice, not a stated rule. Form: 'In writing, and in 12-point font or larger, or other easily legible font' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)(iii)); 'Include telephone number(s) and e-mail of the property manager or other person responsible for accepting reasonable accommodation requests' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)(iv)); 'By email, text, electronic messaging system, facsimile, or hardcopy' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)(v)); a link is permissible 'provided the communication also contains text to inform the prospective tenant that the link contains information regarding' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)(v)) accommodation rights, and 'The notice must be available for printing and downloading' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)(v)); on paper 'the notice must be included within such communication, or by providing the notice in an accompanying document' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)(vi)); it 'May be accomplished by including the notice in or with other written communications, such as a lease or other written materials routinely provided to tenants' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)(vii)). Not enough: 'Oral disclosure does not satisfy the requirements imposed by this section' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)(viii)); '"Posting" of the notice pursuant to paragraph (d)(3) of this subdivision, either on paper, on a bulletin board, or on an electronic bulletin board or notice area, does not satisfy the requirements imposed by this section' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)(ix)). Posting and websites: 'In addition to the delivery of notice in paragraphs (d)(1) and (d)(2) of this subdivision, all housing providers shall post the notice in the following manner' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(3)): 'As required by 9 NYCRR 466.3' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(3)(i)), which requires that notices be 'posted conspicuously' (9 NYCRR 466.3(a)(2)) in 'places at the housing accommodations, where they may be readily observed by those seeking such housing accommodations' (9 NYCRR 466.3(a)(2)); and 'all websites created and maintained by housing providers shall prominently and conspicuously display on the homepage of such website a link to the Division's notice as required this regulation which shall be made available by the Division' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(3)(ii), wording as printed). Brokers: 'A real estate broker shall be responsible to ensure that each individual licensed pursuant to Article 12-A of the New York Real Property Law and associated with such broker provides notice with regard to available housing accommodations' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(2)), 'Upon first substantive contact' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(2)(i)). Content: 'The following shall be deemed sufficient notice when provided to the individual to be notified' (9 NYCRR 466.15(e)), and 'The Notice must include contact information when being provided under 466.15(d)(1), above' (9 NYCRR 466.15(e)). Exemptions: 9 NYCRR 466.15 states none and defines a housing provider in the words of N.Y. Exec. Law § 296(5) and N.Y. Exec. Law § 296(2-a) (9 NYCRR 466.15(c)(1)); whether the owner-occupied two-family and room exemptions of N.Y. Exec. Law § 296(5)(a) carry over is not settled here, and no New York case law was searched (NY gaps log §1). 9 NYCRR 466.15 states no penalty of its own. History on LII: 9 NYCRR 466.15 adopted in the State Register of May 18, 2022, effective that day, with no later amendment shown; 9 NYCRR 466.3 shows no history line. Found by {B:NYG-91} (heading_only screen). Lease vehicle: `accommodation-notice-ny` (REQUIRED_DISCLOSURE). 9 NYCRR Part 466 read 2026-11-15 from Cornell LII's republication of the NYCRR (law.cornell.edu), section history as shown there (NY gaps log §1.5).
```

- verification_status: VERIFIED
- supersedes, choice_group, is_default: (blank)

###### 1b. Lease clause (REQUIRED_DISCLOSURE, lease as a permitted vehicle)

Why a clause: 9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1) requires a written notice to every new tenant within 30 days of the tenancy and says it 'May be accomplished by including the notice in or with other written communications, such as a lease or other written materials routinely provided to tenants' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)(vii)), and 9 NYCRR 466.15(e) prints text that is 'deemed sufficient'. That is a rule requiring text in writing with the lease as a possible vehicle, the same footing as `lead-based-paint` and `good-cause-notice-ny`. It is not a restatement of a term the law implies into every lease, so the standing preference against restating law is not engaged in the usual way, but it is flagged below for Taylor's decision (default REQUIRED versus optional). The clause does not satisfy the posting and website duties or reach existing tenants; its bracket says so and the education row explains them.

Proposed row:

- id: `accommodation-notice-ny`
- group: Disclosures
- title: Notice of Tenants' Rights to Reasonable Accommodations
- content_type: LEASE_CLAUSE
- rule_type: REQUIRED
- lease_clause_basis: REQUIRED_DISCLOSURE: N.Y. Exec. Law § 170-d; 9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1); 9 NYCRR 466.15(e)
- topic_key: disability-accommodation
- bodyText:

```text
[Print this Section in 12-point type or larger. Fill in at least one telephone number and the e-mail address of the property manager or other person who receives reasonable accommodation requests; if there is only one telephone number, delete the second blank and the word before it. The notice is not complete without this contact information. This Section gives a new Tenant the written notice that the New York State Division of Human Rights requires within 30 days after the tenancy begins; Landlord must also post the notice at the property and link to it from the homepage of any website Landlord maintains, which this Section does not do (`edu-accommodation-notice-ny`). Keep the text below as the Division prints it.]
NOTICE DISCLOSING TENANTS' RIGHTS TO REASONABLE ACCOMMODATIONS FOR PERSONS WITH DISABILITIES
Reasonable Accommodations
The New York State Human Rights Law requires housing providers to make reasonable accommodations or modifications to a building or living space to meet the needs of people with disabilities. For example, if you have a physical, mental, or medical impairment, you can ask your housing provider to make the common areas of your building accessible, or to change certain policies to meet your needs.
To request a reasonable accommodation, you should contact your property manager by calling [telephone number] or [second telephone number], or by e-mailing [e-mail address]. You will need to inform your housing provider that you have a disability or health problem that interferes with your use of housing, and that your request for accommodation may be necessary to provide you equal access and opportunity to use and enjoy your housing or the amenities and services normally offered by your housing provider. A housing provider may request medical information, when necessary to support that there is a covered disability and that the need for the accommodation is disability related.
If you believe that you have been denied a reasonable accommodation for your disability, or that you were denied housing or retaliated against because you requested a reasonable accommodation, you can file a complaint with the New York State Division of Human Rights as described at the end of this notice.
Specifically, if you have a physical, mental, or medical impairment, you can request:+
Permission to change the interior of your housing unit to make it accessible (however, you are required to pay for these modifications, and in the case of a rental your housing provider may require that you restore the unit to its original condition when you move out);
Changes to your housing provider's rules, policies, practices, or services;
Changes to common areas of the building so you have an equal opportunity to use the building. The New York State Human Rights Law requires housing providers to pay for reasonable modifications to common use areas.
Examples of reasonable modifications and accommodations that may be requested under the New York State Human Rights Law include:
If you have a mobility impairment, your housing provider may be required to provide you with a ramp or other reasonable means to permit you to enter and exit the building.
If your healthcare provider provides documentation that having an animal will assist with your disability, you should be permitted to have the animal in your home despite a "no pet" rule.
If you need grab bars in your bathroom, you can request permission to install them at your own expense. If your housing was built for first occupancy after March 13, 1991 and the walls need to be reinforced for grab bars, your housing provider must pay for that to be done.
If you have an impairment that requires a parking space close to your unit, you can request your housing provider to provide you with that parking space, or place you at the top of a waiting list if no adjacent spot is available.
If you have a visual impairment and require printed notices in an alternative format such as large print font, or need notices to be made available to you electronically, you can request that accommodation from your landlord.
Required Accessibility Standards
All buildings constructed for use after March 13, 1991, are required to meet the following standards:
Public and common areas must be readily accessible to and usable by persons with disabilities;
All doors must be sufficiently wide to allow passage by persons in wheelchairs; and
All multi-family buildings must contain accessible passageways, fixtures, outlets, thermostats, bathrooms, and kitchens.
If you believe that your building does not meet the required accessibility standards, you can file a complaint with the New York State Division of Human Rights.
How to File a Complaint
A complaint must be filed with the Division within one year of the alleged discriminatory act or in court within three years of the alleged discriminatory act. You can find more information on your rights, and on the procedures for filing a complaint, by going to www.dhr.ny.gov, or by calling 1-888-392-3644. You can obtain a complaint form on the website, or one can be e-mailed or mailed to you. You can also call or e-mail a Division regional office. The regional offices are listed on the website.
+ This Notice provides information about your rights under the New York State Human Rights Law, which applies to persons residing anywhere in New York State. Local laws may provide protections in addition to those described in this Notice, but local laws cannot decrease your protections.
```

- notes:

```text
NY: REQUIRED_DISCLOSURE, lease as vehicle. Every housing provider 'must provide notice, as provided for in this regulation, to all new and current tenants' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)), 'Within 30 days of the effective date of their tenancy' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)(i)) and 'In writing, and in 12-point font or larger, or other easily legible font' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)(iii)), and the notice 'May be accomplished by including the notice in or with other written communications, such as a lease or other written materials routinely provided to tenants' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)(vii)); on paper 'the notice must be included within such communication, or by providing the notice in an accompanying document' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)(vi)). Text: the Division's model notice, reproduced word for word from the regulation, which says 'The following shall be deemed sufficient notice when provided to the individual to be notified' (9 NYCRR 466.15(e)); the blanks take the contact details the rule requires, 'Include telephone number(s) and e-mail of the property manager or other person responsible for accepting reasonable accommodation requests' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(1)(iv)), and 'The Notice must include contact information when being provided under 466.15(d)(1), above' (9 NYCRR 466.15(e)); the regulation's own footnote marker (*) on the contact sentence is dropped because it points to that drafting instruction, while the (+) footnote is part of the notice and kept. Hand-filled brackets, not variables, because the person who takes accommodation requests may differ from the manager the lease names elsewhere. Limits stated in the bracket: the lease does not meet the separate duties to post the notice 'As required by 9 NYCRR 466.3' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(3)(i)) and to link it from 'the homepage of such website' (9 NYCRR 466.15(d)(3)(ii)), or reach tenants already in place (`edu-accommodation-notice-ny`). Rule type REQUIRED, as for `lead-based-paint` and `good-cause-notice-ny`: the rule reaches every New York tenancy and the lease is the surest delivery; it is not a restatement of a term the law implies into the lease. Decision for Taylor: whether the library should instead offer it as optional, given the standing preference against clauses that restate law; it is proposed because the rule requires a written notice and names the lease as a permitted vehicle, and the clause reproduces the prescribed text rather than summarizing the law. Builder: print in 12-point type or larger; the Division may revise its notice, so this row should be re-checked against 9 NYCRR 466.15(e) at each currency pass. 9 NYCRR 466.15 adopted in the State Register of May 18, 2022 per LII, no later amendment shown. 9 NYCRR Part 466 read 2026-11-15 from Cornell LII's republication of the NYCRR (law.cornell.edu), section history as shown there (NY gaps log §1.5).
```

- verification_status: VERIFIED
- supersedes, choice_group, is_default: (blank)

Decision for Taylor (surfaced, not resolved): keep `accommodation-notice-ny` as a default REQUIRED clause, or offer it as CONDITIONAL/optional with the education row carrying the duty. The proposal takes REQUIRED because the rule reaches every New York tenancy and the lease is the surest delivery for a new tenant.

The `required-disclosures` catalog row (`edu-required-disclosures-ny`, this batch) names this notice as a non-lease notice; if `accommodation-notice-ny` is added, that catalog entry should say the notice may go in the lease (`accommodation-notice-ny`) and still must be posted and linked from any website.

###### 1c. Independent check of the proposals (r3)
- The model notice was checked word for word against 9 NYCRR 466.15(e). The only changes are the hand-fill blanks and the dropped "*" drafting footnote; the "+" footnote is kept. The timing (30 days; existing tenants from March 2, 2021) and the 12-point type were confirmed.
- Changes made from the check:
  - The basis now names the statute first (above).
  - The education row should also state the separate duty to post the general Human Rights Law notice, 'subject to the Human Rights Law' (9 NYCRR 466.3(a)(1)), to agree with `edu-no-tenant-rights-statement-ny`. Proposed added sentence: "Separately, you must post the Division of Human Rights' notice of the Human Rights Law at your rental office or property, where it can be seen."
  - As a cautious course (not required by the rule), add to the clause's opening bracket: "If this Lease is signed more than 30 days before the tenancy begins, give this notice again within 30 days after the tenancy begins."

##### 2. `edu-lead-ny` (topic `lead-based-paint`): omits the state lead rental registry (N.Y. Pub. Health Law § 1377)

The row's notes say N.Y. Pub. Health Law §§ 1373 and 1377 were 'not saved or read' (NY log §7 item 13). Both are now in the corpus and were read whole. N.Y. Pub. Health Law § 1377 (revisions listed 2023-05-12, 2023-06-23, 2025-11-07; prints '* NB Repealed November 3, 2028') creates an owner duty the row does not mention:

'Owners of all residential dwellings qualifying for registration in accordance with this section shall certify as free of lead paint hazards, in accordance with subdivision seven of this section and the regulations promulgated thereunder. Inspection certifications must be submitted by the owner to the local health department or their designee for recording in the rental registry by October first of the year the certification is due' (N.Y. Pub. Health Law § 1377(3)); the registry covers 'all residential dwellings with two or more units built prior to nineteen hundred eighty ... located within communities of concern' and 'only include qualifying residential dwellings outside a city with a population of one million people or more' (N.Y. Pub. Health Law § 1377(1)).

Row words: "New York State adds no separate lead disclosure for private rentals; its lead poisoning prevention program is run by the Health Department with local health officers." The first clause stays true (no tenant disclosure; {B:NYG-96}); the second is incomplete. Suggested replacement for that sentence: "New York State adds no separate lead disclosure for private rentals. Outside New York City, owners of pre-1980 buildings with two or more units in a Health Department community of concern must have them inspected and certified free of lead paint hazards (see `edu-lead-safe-certification-ny`); the Health Department and local health officers can also order lead paint conditions corrected." Notes: replace "N.Y. Pub. Health Law §§ 1373 and 1377 (not saved or read; the abatement-order provisions are not relied on)" with "N.Y. Pub. Health Law § 1373 (abatement orders, read 2026-11-15) and N.Y. Pub. Health Law § 1377 (registry and certification, read 2026-11-15; `edu-lead-safe-certification-ny`)".

##### 3. `edu-owner-identity-ny` (topic `owner-identity-disclosure`): "Registration under the Multiple Residence Law was not located"

N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 300 ("Registry of owner", revision 2014-09-22) exists: 'On or before July first, nineteen hundred fifty-four, every owner of a multiple dwelling shall file with the department a statement, in duplicate, on a form to be prepared by the department substantially as follows: Owner of record: ...' (N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 300). It is a one-time 1954 filing, not a continuing registration, so the row's practical advice stands, but the "not located" sentence is wrong. Suggested notes wording: "The Multiple Residence Law's owner registry (N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 300) was a one-time filing due July 1, 1954; it sets no continuing registration or tenant disclosure."

##### 4. `edu-local-registration-ny` (topic `landlord-registration`): no mention of the state lead rental registry

The row covers local registration and inspection laws but not the state registry created by N.Y. Pub. Health Law § 1377 (outside New York City; pre-1980 buildings of two or more units in communities of concern; certification by October 1 of the year due). Suggested added body sentence: "Outside New York City, the state Health Department also keeps a lead rental registry for pre-1980 buildings with two or more units in designated communities of concern, and their owners must file lead-safe certifications (see `edu-lead-safe-certification-ny`)." Notes: "N.Y. Pub. Health Law § 1377(1), (3) (read 2026-11-15; repealed November 3, 2028)."

##### 5. Place-name rule with no row: N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 396-d

'Whenever any person or persons, firm or corporation, shall use in connection with the designation, description, advertisement, lease or sale of any real property, any name which shall include the name of any political or territorial subdivision situated in the county or in any county immediately adjoining the county in which such property is situated, other than the name of the political and territorial subdivision in which the particular property referred to is located, such person or persons, firm or corporation shall, as part of every such designation, description, advertisement, lease or sale, and with the same prominence given to any such name or part of such name, state and display the name of the town, village, city or other political subdivision in which such property is actually located' (N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 396-d; revision 2014-09-22). Found by the completeness screen {B:NYG-103}. It is now named in `edu-required-disclosures-ny` (this batch). If the main pass wants a clause, a CONDITIONAL lease clause could read: "[Use only if this Lease or the listing describes the Premises by the name of a city, town or village other than the one where it is located.] The Premises is located in the [town, village or city] of [name]." with lease_clause_basis "REQUIRED_DISCLOSURE: N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 396-d", group Disclosures, topic_key required-disclosures; the builder would print it with the same prominence as the other place name. Not written here because the topic's catalog row covers it and the rule applies only where a different place name is used.

`smoke-detector-duties-ny` carries lease_clause_basis "REQUIRED_DISCLOSURE: N.Y. Mult. Dwell. Law § 68(4)(c); N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 15(4)(c)" and is included in `edu-required-disclosures-ny`. No change to the row is needed.

#### From F_utilities_building

#### Findings on existing rows (batch F_utilities_building)

##### 1. `edu-utility-submetering-ny`: first sentence overstates the absence; N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 52 not stated
The row says: "No New York statute sets rules for a landlord who submeters or allocates utility costs to tenants of a market-rate building."
N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 52 is a statute on one kind of allocation: where a tenant is the utility's customer of record for gas, electric or steam service through a meter that 'also measures such service to areas outside that dwelling' (N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 52(1)(b)), 'An owner shall eliminate any shared meter condition or, in the alternative, establish an account in the' owner name 'for all the shared area charges for service measured through a shared meter' (N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 52(2)(a)); the section 'may not be waived by an owner, tenant, or utility' (N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 52(3)(a)). Article 2 also reaches 'any entity that, in any manner, sells or facilitates the sale or furnishing of gas or electricity to residential customers' (N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 53), and N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 66-m(3) shows the commission approves 'the conversion to submetering of any master meter'.
Proposed wording for the first sentence: "No New York statute sets rules for splitting a master-metered bill you pay yourself among tenants, but a shared meter on a tenant's own gas, electric or steam account is regulated by statute (`edu-utility-apportionment-ny`), and electric submetering needs Public Service Commission approval under its rules (`edu-electric-submetering-ny`)."
Proposed notes addition: "N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 52 (shared meters) and N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 53 (article 2 reaches any entity that sells or facilitates the sale of gas or electricity to residential customers) read 2026-11-15 (NY gaps log, batch F); see `edu-utility-apportionment-ny`, `edu-electric-submetering-ny`."

##### 2. `edu-utility-shutoff-ny`: the Public Service Law notice procedure is now read and should be stated
The row's notes say "Utility notice procedures in the Public Service Law were not read (NY log §7)." N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law §§ 33 and 34 were read whole on 2026-11-15 (revision 2014-09-22 for both). They give the tenants the payment route the row's offset sentence depends on:
- 'no public utility company or municipality shall discontinue gas, electric or steam service to an entire multiple dwelling (as defined in the multiple dwelling law or the multiple residence law) located anywhere in this state for nonpayment of bills rendered for service unless such utility shall have given fifteen days written notice of its intention so to discontinue' (N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 33(1)), posted in the public areas and mailed to the "Occupant" of each unit (N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 33(1)(c)); commission rules 'provide that utility service may not be discontinued to any such multiple dwelling as long as the tenants continue to make timely payments in accordance with established procedures' and 'assure that tenants shall not be liable for bills more than two months in arrears' (N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 33(5)); water is the same under N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 116.
- two-family dwellings without separate meters: notice to each occupied unit, and 'Any occupant who chooses to pay current charges shall not be liable for any future bills which may be rendered for utility service supplied to the dwelling' (N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 34(1), N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 34(3)(b)).
Proposed body sentence to add after the first sentence: "Before a utility shuts off service to a whole building of three or more apartments, or to a two-family house without separate meters, for your nonpayment, it must give every occupant 15 days' written notice of how tenants can pay to keep service, and tenants who pay current charges are not liable for more than two months of your arrears (`edu-utility-landlord-account-ny`)."
Proposed notes change: replace "Utility notice procedures in the Public Service Law were not read (NY log §7)." with "Utility notice procedures: N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 33(1), (5), N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 34(1), (3)(b), N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 116, read 2026-11-15 (NY gaps log batch F; `edu-utility-landlord-account-ny`); the commission's implementing rules (16 NYCRR) not read."

#### From G_alterations_access_misc

#### Findings on existing rows: batch G_alterations_access_misc (2026-11-15)

##### `edu-no-ev-charging-rule-ny` (topic ev-charging)
- The NY note calls N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 339-ll "heading-only". In the current corpus it is a body hit (NYG-140, term t1) and is the Condominium Act's charger rule: 'Any covenant, restriction, or condition contained in any deed, contract, security instrument, or other instrument affecting the transfer or sale of any interest in the property, and any by-laws, that either effectively prohibits or unreasonably restricts the installation or use of an electric vehicle charging station within an owner's unit or in a designated parking space' is 'void and unenforceable' (N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 339-ll(2)(a)).
- The body says New York has no statute "requiring a landlord to provide one". N.Y. Energy Law § 11-104(9)(b) directs that any energy code adopted 'after December thirty-first, two thousand twenty-six' require 'any new building where new off-street parking is associated with such new building to have electric vehicle ready infrastructure and electric vehicle charging stations', not applying to existing buildings (N.Y. Energy Law § 11-104(9)(c)). The body also uses the "New York has no statute" form that class decision 14 avoids.
- Proposed body: "No section of the Consolidated Laws of New York or the New York Constitution gives a tenant the right to install an electric vehicle charger or requires a landlord to provide one at an existing building. Any charging arrangement is up to the lease. New buildings with new off-street parking will need EV-ready wiring and charging stations under the state energy code adopted after December 31, 2026 (`edu-no-ev-charging-requirements-ny`)."
- Proposed note addition: "N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 339-ll is the condominium unit-owner rule, a body hit in {B:NYG-140}; N.Y. Energy Law § 11-104(9)(b)-(d) (new buildings)."

##### `edu-towing-ny` (topic towing) and `parking-vehicle-rules-ny` (notes)
- Both say the only state towing rule is N.Y. Veh. & Traf. Law § 1210(c) ("the one state rule"; "no other state statute sets when a landlord may tow from a residential lot"). NYG-151 found N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 399-v (revision 2025-08-01), which neither row cites: a 'parking facility' is one 'having a parking capacity of five or more motor vehicles not owned or operated by the state, a municipality or public authority' (N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 399-v(1)(c)); 'No owner or operator of a parking facility shall tow or authorize the towing of any motor vehicle or immobilize or authorize the immobilization of any motor vehicle in such parking facility unless such owner or operator displays a notice pursuant to subdivision two of this section' (N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 399-v(3)); no payment from a commercial tower (N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 399-v(4)); 'The provisions of this section shall not apply to cities having a population of one million or more' (N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 399-v(5)); damages of 'actual damages or one hundred fifty dollars, whichever is greater', up to treble and one thousand dollars, and attorney's fees (N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 399-v(6)).
- Proposed `edu-towing-ny` body: "New York State has two towing rules for private lots. Outside New York City, a private parking lot with room for five or more vehicles must display a conspicuous notice with the operator's and the authorized tow or boot company's names, addresses and phone numbers, stating that unauthorized vehicles will be towed at the owner's expense; without that notice you may not tow or boot, and you may not accept any payment from the tow company. A towing company that removes a vehicle unlawfully parked on private property at the owner's request, without the vehicle owner's written consent, must immediately notify the local police. Local laws (including New York City's and several counties') add more. Post the rules, follow the local law, and do not use towing to force a tenant out."
- Proposed note addition (both rows): "N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 399-v(1)-(7) (posting before towing or booting from a private lot of five or more spaces outside cities of one million or more; {B:NYG-151}; `edu-parking-rules-notice-ny`)." The clause text of `parking-vehicle-rules-ny` ("in accordance with applicable law") stays lawful; only its note is incomplete.

##### `edu-security-devices-ny` (citation form)
- The note cites "N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 390-e" without its heading. Two sections share the number (the corpus prints "NB There are 2 § 390-e's"); the citation format requires `N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 390-e (keyless security devices)`. Proposed: replace each bare "N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 390-e" in the note with "N.Y. Gen. Bus. Law § 390-e (keyless security devices)".

- `smoking-policy` (central edit 2026-10-04): New York doesn't use the base clause; `smoking-policy-ny` replaces it (N.Y. Cannabis Law § 127(2)). Confirmed, with one wording finding on `smoking-policy-ny` below.

Its last sentence ("does not limit any repair, installation, rekeying, or reasonable modification that applicable law entitles Tenant to perform") keeps the reasonable modifications of N.Y. Exec. Law § 296(18), the tenant's own lock in a multiple dwelling (N.Y. Mult. Dwell. Law § 51-c) and the cable installations N.Y. Pub. Serv. Law § 228 protects (`edu-telecom-access-ny`). No New York statute gives a tenant a right to install solar equipment, an EV charger or a portable cooling device (the batch G rows record the batteries), so the consent requirement doesn't take away a statutory right.
- `addendum-precedence`: confirmed. Its exception ("any disclosure, notice, or addendum that applicable law requires, or that applicable law says controls over this Lease, will control") covers every New York notice the library requires: `sprinkler-disclosure-ny` (N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 231-a), `flood-disclosure-ny` (N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 231-b), `good-cause-notice-ny` (N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 231-c), `certificate-of-occupancy-notice-ny` (N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 235-bb(1)), `deposit-bank-notice-ny` (N.Y. Gen. Oblig. Law § 7-103(2)), `emergency-contact-consent-ny` (N.Y. Mult. Dwell. Law § 15(2); N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 16(2)) and the federal lead disclosure (FEDERAL: 42 U.S.C. § 4852d). The NY segment names only the first three plus lead. Oblig. Law § 7-103(2); `deposit-bank-notice-ny`) and the emergency-contact notice (N.Y. Mult. Dwell. Law § 15(2), N.Y. Mult. Resid. ###### Finding: `smoking-policy-ny` should name whose smoking Tenant pays for
- Row says: "Tenant will be responsible for any cost Landlord incurs to remediate odor, staining or damage caused by smoking in violation of this Section".
- New York ties a tenant's responsibility for a condition to named persons: 'When any such condition has been caused by the misconduct of the tenant or lessee or persons under his direction or control, it shall not constitute a breach of such covenants and warranties' (N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 235-b(1)); the tenant 'shall be liable if a violation is caused by his own wilful act, assistance or negligence or that of any member of his family or household or his guest' (N.Y. Mult. Dwell. Law § 78(1)); and 'any member of his family or household or his guests' (N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 174). As written the sentence could reach smoke from a neighbour or a stranger, which none of these statutes puts on the tenant.
- Proposed wording (matches the 2026-10-04 central edit of `smoking-policy`): "Tenant will be responsible for any cost Landlord incurs to remediate odor, staining or damage caused by smoking by Tenant, an occupant, or a guest or invitee of Tenant in violation of this Section, and a violation may be treated as a default under this Lease."
- Proposed note addition: "NY: The cost sentence names Tenant, an occupant, or a guest or invitee of Tenant, the persons for whom New York makes a tenant answerable (N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 235-b(1); N.Y. Mult. Dwell. Law § 78(1); N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 174)."

##### Item 9: `hoa-compliance`
Fits as tagged; no `-ny` override. N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 238-a(1)(a) bars a 'payment, fee or charge' demanded 'for the processing, review or acceptance of an application' or 'before or at the beginning of the tenancy'; a fine the association levies during the tenancy for Tenant's rule breach is neither. N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 234-a(a) bars 'any fee, surcharge or other charges for legal services' without a court order; the clause passes on "fines" only, so it doesn't carry the association's legal fees. One limit for the sync note (no text change): 'No fees, charges or penalties other than rent may be sought in a summary proceeding' (N.Y. Real Prop. Acts. Law § 702(1)), so a passed-through fine is collected outside the eviction case. Proposed NY note: "NY: Applies as written. A fine the association levies during the tenancy for Tenant's breach is not an application or move-in charge barred by N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 238-a(1)(a), and the clause does not pass on legal fees (N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 234-a). A fine cannot be sought in a summary proceeding (N.Y. Real Prop. Acts. Law § 702(1))."

##### Item 10(a): proposed `early-termination-ny` (supersedes `early-termination-ks` for New York)
- id: early-termination-ny
- group: Default & Termination
- title: Early Termination
- content_type: LEASE_CLAUSE
- rule_type: RECOMMENDED (Taylor, 2026-11-12)
- lease_clause_basis: SERVES_LANDLORD
- topic_key: early-termination
- supersedes: early-termination-ks
- choice_group / is_default: blank
- verification_status: VERIFIED
- bodyText: "Tenant may terminate this Lease before the end of the Term by providing Landlord at least 30 days' written notice. Tenant will pay Landlord, by the termination date, an early termination fee equal to one month's Rent ({{monthly_rent}}), and remains responsible for Rent and other obligations up to the termination date. If Tenant gives that notice, pays the fee and delivers possession of the property by the termination date, Tenant owes no Rent for any period after the termination date. If Tenant leaves before the end of the Term without using this option, Tenant's liability is governed by New York law, including Landlord's duty to try to re-rent the property. This early-termination option and fee apply only if this Lease has a fixed Term; a month-to-month or other periodic tenancy ends on the notice that applicable law and this Lease provide, without an early termination fee. Landlord may terminate this Lease early only as this Lease's Tenant Default and notice provisions and New York law allow, and will regain possession only if Tenant surrenders it or through a court proceeding. Nothing in this Section limits any right either party has under applicable law, including a Tenant's right to terminate because of military service under the Servicemembers Civil Relief Act, as a victim of domestic violence, or as a senior citizen or person with a disability moving to care or to a family member's home, the rights of a deceased Tenant's estate, or a Tenant's right to surrender a residence made untenantable by a casualty Tenant did not cause."
- notes: "NY: Replaces `early-termination-ks` for New York (flat one-month fee, as `early-termination-mo`, `early-termination-id`, `early-termination-in`, `early-termination-mi`, `early-termination-ia` and `early-termination-nm`). No New York statute caps or bars an agreed early-termination option (NY battery 31); a fee far above the landlord's likely loss could be challenged as a penalty, which only case law settles (`edu-no-early-termination-fee-rule-ny`; no New York case law was searched, NY gaps log §1), and a one-month fee is the cautious course. A tenant who uses the option does not vacate 'in violation of the terms of the lease', so the mitigation duty does not reduce the agreed fee (N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 227-e). N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 238-a(1)(a) bars payments demanded 'before or at the beginning of the tenancy'; a fee the tenant chooses to pay on exit is not one. The landlord limb of `early-termination-ks` ('if Tenant vacates or abandons the property without notifying Landlord') is dropped: 'No tenant or lawful occupant of a dwelling or housing accommodation shall be removed from possession except in a special proceeding' (N.Y. Real Prop. Acts. Law § 711); it is unlawful to evict an occupant of 30 days or more 'except to the extent permitted by law pursuant to a warrant of eviction or other order of a court of competent jurisdiction or a governmental vacate order', including by 'changing the lock on such entrance door without supplying the occupant with a key' (N.Y. Real Prop. Acts. Law § 768(1)(a)(iii)), with civil penalties of 'not less than one thousand nor more than ten thousand dollars for each violation' (N.Y. Real Prop. Acts. Law § 768(2)(b)); New York has no abandonment statute (`edu-abandoned-property-ny`); and where the Good Cause Eviction Law applies, removal needs a court order on a listed ground (N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 216(1)), none of which is vacating without notice. The saving sentence keeps the statutory exits: N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 227-c (domestic violence), N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 227-a (seniors and persons with a disability), N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 236-a (a deceased tenant's estate), N.Y. Real Prop. Law § 227 (casualty without the tenant's fault, subject to `casualty-termination-ny`) and FEDERAL: 50 U.S.C. § 3955 (`edu-servicemember-ny`)."
- Claude Code applies this at sync (NY gaps prompt item 10(a)).

##### `edu-heat-ny`: the Multiple Residence Law's own heat rule is missing (from the independent check, r1-s1 NOTE)
- Row says: "Local codes may set the rules elsewhere."
- Statute: 'Every new dwelling shall be provided with heat in all living rooms sufficient to maintain the minimum temperatures required by local law, ordinances, rules or regulation, or by the local public health officer, provided, however, that such minimum temperature shall ... be sixty-eight degrees Fahrenheit during the hours between six o'clock in the morning and ten o'clock in the evening during the months between October first and May thirty-first, whenever the outdoor temperature falls below fifty-five degrees Fahrenheit' (N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 173). It sits in article five, which applies 'to every multiple dwelling of permanent or transient occupancy erected on or after July first, nineteen hundred fifty-two' (N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 100). The Multiple Residence Law applies to 'all cities of less than three hundred twenty-five thousand population and to all towns and villages' (N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 3(1)).
- Proposed wording (replaces that sentence): "Elsewhere, a multiple dwelling built on or after July 1, 1952 that the Multiple Residence Law covers must have heat in all living rooms keeping at least 68°F from 6 a.m. to 10 p.m., October 1 through May 31, whenever it is below 55°F outside; local law may set further minimums."
- Proposed note addition: "Multiple Residence Law: N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 173, applied by N.Y. Mult. Resid. Law § 100 to multiple dwellings erected on or after July 1, 1952."

##### 1. `tenant-caused-fines-as` carve-out (17 states tagged)

The clause ends: "This Section does not apply to a fine or charge for a condition Landlord is responsible for under this Lease or the law". In a state whose housing code keeps the owner responsible for compliance while also making the tenant liable for tenant-caused violations (New York's pattern: N.Y. Mult. Dwell. Law § 78(1), 'The owner shall be responsible for compliance with the provisions of this section; but the tenant also shall be liable if a violation is caused by his own wilful act, assistance or negligence'), the carve-out can be read to exclude every tenant-caused code violation, because the landlord is still "responsible ... under ... the law". Ohio already overrode the clause on a related ground (`tenant-caused-fines-oh`). Worth a check in each tagged state whose code has a dual-liability sentence; the New York row `governmental-fines-ny` carves out only conditions Landlord caused. Not researched for any other state.

No other problems in other states' rows were noticed.

### Proposed SOP changes

1. **Signed-bill screens on nysenate.gov (rule 16):** read signing dates from each bill page's action line ("signed chap. N", with its date), not from the search listing. The listing card's date is a status or milestone date: A10338 shows "February 24, 2026" on the listing, but its page reads "Jun 26, 2026 signed chap.137". Several actions can share one date line ("Oct 28, 2026 approval memo.6 signed chap.368"), so take the nearest preceding date. Read "Laws Affected" to the summary marker, not to a fixed length; omnibus bills run to thousands of characters. Check that the chapter numbers in the screened window have no gaps.
2. **Built-in browser crawls (rule 12):** a hidden pane throttles page timers (setTimeout chains slowed from about 87 to about 4 pages a minute). Pace a long crawl or fetch loop with a Web Worker timer.
3. **Export channel:** don't pad small exports with repeated filler to force them to a file; a safety classifier stopped a helper for it. A result over about 70,000 characters is saved to a file on its own. A result well under that comes back inline and is read from the transcript.
4. **Battery ids (rule 19):** when a later pass for a state runs new batteries, give them a distinct prefix (here NYG-n), and cite the earlier pass's as "NY log battery N". The delta mixes "NY battery 62" (October) and "NYG-62" (this pass), which a checker flagged as easy to confuse.
5. **Assembler (rule 80):** fail on any unfilled or malformed battery placeholder (`{{B:`, `NYG-{n}`), not only on unknown ids. One row shipped with a literal placeholder before the check caught it; the check is now in the tool.
6. **Multiple Dwelling Law place limit (rule 27 [AK 3]):** both check slices found Multiple Dwelling Law rules stated with no place limit (14 rows). Make it a canvass-brief item: every row resting on a Multiple Dwelling Law section names the place limit in the body (cities of 325,000 or more, and adopting localities) and the Multiple Residence Law counterpart where one exists, or says there is none.

### Proposed topic questions

- `communal-land-lease-approval`: the topic reads as a territory topic. New York's Indian Law has approval and void-lease rules for leases of nation lands (Seneca, Onondaga, Tonawanda; the Salamanca village leases). Proposed question: "Does state law require approval of, or void, leases of land held by a recognized Indian nation or tribe within the state, and does that reach residential leases of such land?"
- `utility-disclosure-attachment`: proposed question: "Where the tenant will pay for heating or cooling, must a landlord give a prospective tenant, on request, past heating or cooling bills or energy-use data for the unit? (New York, N.Y. Energy Law § 17-103(1)(c): two years of bills, free of charge.)"
- `lead-safe-certification`: proposed question: "Does a state statute outside the largest city require periodic lead-safe certification or registration of older rental buildings, and on what schedule? (New York, N.Y. Pub. Health Law § 1377, repealed November 3, 2028.)"
